[section 13]

## The Particle a)/n

## Its use and etymology

§423

The particle
a)/n is largely used to
color the moods of the Greek language.

§424

Distinctions in the use of a)/n.

Two sets of distinctions are necessary as to the use of the particle

a)/n.

I. 1. It may be used in the leading clause, chiefly with the
indicative and the optative or their representatives.

2. It may be used in a dependent sentence, chiefly with the
subjunctive.

II. 1. It may be used with a definite reference, contained in the
same sentence, or implied in the context.

2. It may be used without a definite reference, in which case no
definite ellipsis is to be supplied.

§425

Shifting from definite to indefinite.

The shifting from definite to indefinite is not peculiar to the
particle
a)/n. Other simple
demonstratives change in the same way. Compare
to\n kai\ to/n, ta\ kai\ ta/, and notice especially

te/ws and
e(/ws.
te/ws is used more frequently without its correlative

e(/ws than with it, so
long being for a while, and
e(/ws is sometimes employed after the same
fashion. [note: A. J. P. iv (1883), 418
note.]

§426

ken (ke), ka.

A similar function is exercised in epic and lyric poetry by
ken (
ke), and
in Doric by
ka. The two particles
a)/n and
ken
are sometimes combined as
a)/n ken. A
common origin once suspected is now seldom maintained. [note: See A. J. P. iii (1882), 446 foll.] The
accented
a)/n may originally have been
more clearly demonstrative, the unaccented
ken more surely indefinite, but the whole matter is
obscure, and a sharp discrimination between
a)/n and
ken often attempted
has never been successfully established.
a)/n is everywhere distinctly preferred in negative
sentences, enters more readily into close combinations, and on
common ground gradually thrusts
ken to
the wall. So in Pindar
a)/n nearly
balances
ken, whereas in Homer
ken greatly preponderates,
ken being to
a)/n in the Iliad as 4 to 1.

§427

Etymology of a)/n.

The etymology of
a)/n is still unsettled.
With a definite reference it may be translated then, in that
case, or, when opposition is implied,
else. (Compare Lat. an.）
Without definite reference, it sometimes gives a potential coloring,
and in combination with the subjunctive and the optative is little
more than a sharper future.
ken (
ka) is also an unsolved riddle.

## Indicative with a)/n

§428

The particle
a)/n belongs to that which
is other than the present, and is found only in past and future
relations.

§429

Unreal indicative with a)/n.

The past tenses of the indicative with
a)/n may denote unreality (the most common use) in such a
way that the imperfect denotes opposition to a continued action
either in the present or in the past; the aorist denotes opposition
to attainment, chiefly in the past, very rarely in the present; and
the pluperfect indicative with
a)/n
denotes opposition to completion, more frequently in the present.

Imperfect in opposition to present:

e)gw\ ga\r . . . ei) me\n mh\ w)/|mhn h(/cein . .
. par' a)nqrw/pous teteleuthko/tas a)mei/nous tw=n e)nqa/de,
h)di/koun a)\n [I should be in the wrong (I
am not)]
ou)k a)ganaktw=n tw=| qana/tw|,
PLATO , Phaedo, 63

Imperfect in opposition to the past:

me/nein ga\r e)ch=n tw=| kathgorou=nti tw=n
a)/llwn, ei) de\ tou=t' e)poi/ei e(/kastos, e)ni/kwn
a)/n [they would have been victorious (they
were not)],
DEM.3.17.

Aorist in opposition to the past:

ei) to\ kai\ to\ e)poi/hsen a(/nqrwpos, ou)k a)\n
a)pe/qanen,
DEM.18.243;
If the man had done so and so, he would not have
died (he did die).

Aorist in opposition to present:

ei) me\n ou)=n a)/nqrwpos, o(\n dei= po/ll'
a)kou=sai kai\ kaka/, |
au)to\s h)=n e)/ndhlos, ou)k a)\n a)ndro\s e)mnh/sqhn
fi/lou [I should not mention the name of a
friend (as I am doing)], AR. Eq. 1276-7.

Pluperfect in opposition to present completion:

ei) de/ ge mhdei\s a)/llos h)\ *zeu=cis e)/grafe,
kalw=s a)/n soi a)peke/krito;
PLATO, Gorg. 453D; But if there were no other
painter than Zeuxis, would your reply hold good?

Pluperfect in opposition to past completion:

ei) . . . o( a)nh\r . . . a)pe/qanen . . .
dikai/ws . . . a)\n e)teqnh/kei, ANTIPHON, 4,
b, 3; If the man had been killed, he
would have been justly killed (his death would have been
justifiable).

For further examples, see Unreal Conditional Sentences.

For
e)boulo/mhn (
h)/qelon)
a)/n with
infinitive antithetical to the dependent verb, see 367.

§430

Indicative with a)/n as potential
of the past.

The indicative of the historical tenses with
a)/n also serves to express potentiality, or guarded
assertion, in the past, chiefly with the ideal second person or

tis, but by no means limited to it.

Ellipses are often easily supplied, but are not necessary. The
translation is freer than in an elliptical conditional sentence. The
protasis is sometimes contained in a participle or otherwise
intimated.

e)/gnw tis a)/n, XEN. Cyr. 3.3.70;
One would (could, might) have known.

o)li/gous a)\n ei)=des, Hell. 6.4.16;
Few should you have seen.

§432

a)/n with the future indicative
and its representatives.

The future indicative with a)/n,
theoretically a legitimate construction, has been kept out of use by
the optative with a)/n and by
a)/n with the subjunctive, and
was counted a solecism even in antiquity. [note: LUCIAN, III, 555 R. B. L. G. on [JUSTIN MART. ], Ep. ad Diogn.
2.4.] A number of instances occur, however, in MSS and
editions, but most of them have been corrected, or are easily
corrigible. The same is true of the representatives of the
indicative, the future optative, infinitive, and participle.

Sometimes there is an anacoluthon, as in
PLATO, Apol. 29C. Sometimes the future indicative is
confounded with the aorist optative, as
PLATO, Euthyd. 275A:

protre/yete for
protre/yaite, the future infinitive with the aorist
infinitive, as
PLATO, Phaedr. 227B:

poih/sesqai for
poih/sasqai, the future participle with the aorist
participle, as
PLATO, Apol. 30B:

poih/sontos for
poih/santos. Sometimes
a)/n
is for
a)n- or
a)na- and belongs to the verb, as
AESCHIN.3.155:

ti/ pot' a)\n e)rei=; Cf. PIND. N. 7.68
and A. J. P. III (1882), 452. Sometimes there is confusion between
present and future infinitive, as in XEN. An. 2.3.18:
e(/cein for
e)/xein. Sometimes there is confusion between
a)/n and
dh/, [note: See Cobet, N. L. 501; Blass,
Mus. 36.221; H.
Richards, Rev. vi (1892), 338.] and easy corrections are
almost always at hand. [note: Famous is
PLATO, Rpb. 615D:

ou)x h(/kei, fa/nai, ou)d' a)\n h(/cei
deu=ro, where
h(/coi
cannot stand, and where
ou)d' a)\n
h(/koi would mean he can't have come. Here
Richards applies his panacea
dh/,
but if ever
a)/n was needed with the
future indicative, it is needed here.] This being the
case, it is not worth while to multiply examples, most of which have
disappeared from critical texts.

HOM. Il. 1.139:
o(\ de/ ken kexolw/setai, o(/n
ken i(/kwmai. 523:
e)moi\ de/ ke
tau=ta melh/setai, o)/fra tele/ssw. 3.138:
tw=| de/ ke nikh/santi fi/lh keklh/sh|

kai/ ke/ tis w(=d'

§433

Non-use of a)/n with present and
perfect indicative.

The present and perfect indicative are not used with
a)/n, but owing to the great variation in
the position of
a)/n beginners sometimes
make a mistake in this regard. In ANDOC. 1.117 read
bou/loisqe for
bou/lesqe. In
PLATO, Legg.
712E, read with Schanz
a)nerwthqei/s (cf. 793 A).

## Optative with a)/n

§434

Potential optative.

The optative with
a)/n is the
potential [note: Objections have been raised to the
term potential, but nothing better has been suggested.
Potentiality has to do with character, not with possibility
merely, and the estimate of character goes back to the opinion
of the one who makes the estimate. Compare A. J. P. xix (1898),
231.] of the Greek language. It is used mainly in leading
clauses. It expresses the opinion of the speaker as an opinion, and
may be called the mood of qualified assertion. The verification of
the opinion or assertion is postponed to another time (
a)/n).

§435

Time and tenses of the potential optative.

Both action and ascertainment may be future, or only the
ascertainment. The present is often used of the future ascertainment
of a present action, far more rarely of the future ascertainment of
a past state. The aorist is very seldom used of the future
ascertainment of a past action. The simple perfect optative with

a)/n is found chiefly in those verbs
in which the perfect is equivalent to the present. In the
periphrastic perfect optative with
a)/n,
the participle is often treated almost like an adjective.

§436

a)/n with the Present Optative not
of the Past: used of what will be, or what will prove to be.

§441

a)/n with the future optative.

The future optative with
a)/n is not in
use. It could arise only from the future indicative with
a)/n, and the future indicative with
a)/n had gone out, if it had ever come in,
before the future optative came in. Still it is found in many texts,
and is not to be discarded in the later time. [note: B,
L. G. on [JUSTIN MART.],
Ep. ad Diogn. 2.4.]

§442

Translation of optative with a)/n.

The optative with
a)/n varies in tone
from strong assurance (must) to faint presumption
(might). [note: A. J. P. xiv (1893),
499, xix (1898), 231.]
May be is often a convenient rendering for the
positive, can't for the negative. Even
shall and will sometimes serve to
reproduce the impression, while would and
should give the regular translation in formulated
conditional sentences. So far from necessarily denoting uncertainty,
it is the combination most frequently used to indicate moral
certainty, and sometimes serves as a climax to the indicative.
Especially common is the aorist optative with
a)/n to express total negation, which cannot be brought
out so well by the future indicative. [note:
JUSTIN MARTYR, Apol. 1.4.10.]

fau=loi . . . a)\n tw=| ge sw=| lo/gw|
ei)=en,

according to your account (437).

§444

a)/n with the optative combined
with the indicative.

combination with the indicative, sometimes as a climax, giving, as
it does, the warmth of personal conviction.

§445

The optative with a)/n in
questions.

The optative with
a)/n in the question
expects the optative with
a)/n in the
answer. The speaker virtually answers himself, shows his own
opinion, or his own desire.

§447

Optative with a)/n in dependent
discourse.

The optative with
a)/n is less frequently
used in dependent discourse, chiefly in relative, interrogative, and
conditional sentences. In these combinations, the optative with

a)/n is often a semi-quotation or
reference to a known or imagined state of mind.

e(/kasto/s ti u(mw=n e)/xei pro\s o(\ bou/loito
a)/n me prw=ton a)pologei=sqai,
ANDOC.1.8; Each of you has some point which he
would wish me to meet first in my defence.

bouleuo/menoi *qhbai=oi o(/pws a)\n th\n
h(gemoni/an la/boien th=s *(ella/dos, XEN. Hell. 7.1.33;
The Thebans planning how they could (saying:
pw=s a)\n la/boimen; how can we?)
gain the primacy of Greece.

ei) me\n ou)=n a)/llous e)/xete oi(=stisin a)\n
doi/hte au)tou/s (sc.
tou\s
i(/ppous), . . .
e)kei/nois di/dote: ei)
me/ntoi h(ma=s a)\n bou/loisqe parasta/tas ma/lista e)/xein,
h(mi=n au)tou\s do/te, XEN. Cyr. 4.5.47; If you
have others to whom you would give the horses, offer them to
them; if, however, you would like most to have us as your
stand-bys, give them to us.

For other examples, see Relative, Conditional, and
Interrogative Sentences.

§448

Adherescent a)/n.

Carefully to be distinguished from these semi-quotations are those
instances in which the
a)/n adheres to
the leading particle and yet the subjunctive is changed into the
optative. This is really anacoluthic and does not count.

tou/tous de\ e)pimelei=sqai th=s po/lews, e(/ws
a)\n (del. Dobr.;
au)= W.)

oi( no/moi teqei=en,
ANDOC.1.81; These were to take
charge of the citizens until the laws should be made. (If

a)/n goes with
teqei=en: until such time as in all likelihood
the laws would be made.）

ANDOC.1.81(see above).

kai/ ke gunh\ fe/roi a)/xqos,
e)pei/ ken a)nh\r a)naqei/h.

Examples will be given under the different categories.

§449

e)a/n, o(/tan, etc., with the
optative.

The particles which coalesce with
a)/n,
such as
e)a/n, o(/tan, o(po/tan,
e)peida/n, rarely forget their belongings, and in good Greek
the optative is more than suspicious.

§450

Omission of a)/n with the
optative.—the pure optative as a potential.

The pure optative (optative without
a)/n）
is sometimes used in the early language as a potential, the negative
being
ou). The instances cited, however,
are not all free from doubt, and outside of these early examples all
pure optatives used as potentials are more or less open to
suspicion. Sometimes
a)/n has been
dropped by haplography, sometimes there has been a confusion with
other words, an
au)= (
*a*u) or a
dh/
(
*d*h), sometimes the
a)/n is to be understood from a preceding
passage, or anticipated from a succeeding passage, sometimes euphony
may have prompted the omission, [note: See A. J. P.
xii, 387. AESCHYL. Cho. 595.
SOPH. Ant.
604-5. THEOGN. 125:
ou/de\ ga\r
ei)dei/hs a)ndro\s no/on, where edd.
ou) ga\r a)\n ei)dei/hs.]
although the Greeks generally are not very sensitive to the
cacophony of recurrent sounds, [note: PINDAR, I. E.
cxiv.

AR. Nub. 776:

o(/pws a)postre/yais a)\n a)/ntidi/kwn
di/khn
. Still notice the rarity of compounds in
a)/nan-.] or the ear may have
been cheated, sometimes the syntax of the editors may have been at
fault, and the optative being equivalent to an imperative may be
considered as an equivalent to the optative with
a)/n. [note: B. L. G. on PIND. O. 3.45:

keino\s ei)/hn. P. 10.21-2:

qeo\s ei)/h |
a)ph/mwn ke/ar.] Many passages
once freely cited have been corrected by editors, sometimes
unnecessarily, and the tendency is decidedly towards the norm.
Similar is the case of the omitted
ken
(
ke), and there are not a few
passages in which the presence of a
ge
suggests a restoration of the regular modal construction.

## Subjunctive with a)/n

§451

The subjunctive with
a)/n is not employed
as a form of independent statement in Attic prose. In dependent
clauses it is either a future or good for all time.

§452

Subjunctive with ken or a)/n as a form of independent statement.

The subjunctive with
ken or
a)/n is occasionally used in HOMER as a
form of independent statement. The negative is
ou).

e)gw\ de/ ken au)to\s e(/lwmai, HOM. Il.
1.137; I will take it myself.

ou)k a)/n toi xrai/smh| ki/qaris, 3.54;
Of no avail to thee shall the cithern be.

The aorist is the tense used in all the above examples, and all
except two are negative.

§456

Omission of a)/n in subjunctive
dependent clauses.

Subjunctive dependent clauses, outside of the pure final sentence,
regularly take
a)/n except in the older
language. The omission of
a)/n is
sometimes due to clerical error, sometimes perhaps to a sense of
euphony, sometimes to a survival of the older construction. See
Final, Temporal, Conditional, and Relative
Sentences.

§457

a)/n with other moods.

The imperative with
a)/n does not occur.
When
a)/n is used with an infinitive, or
participle, the clause must be resolved by an indicative or an
optative, according to the context.

§459

Position of a)/n and ke（n).

a)/n and
ke（
n) are both
postpositive. Both are apt to combine with some leading modifier,
conjunction, relative, adverb, only certain monosyllabic particles,
such as
me/n, de/, ga/r, te, being
allowed to intervene.
a)/n sometimes
goes so far as to coalesce sooner or later. So we find
ei) a)/n (
h)/n, e)a/n,
a_)/n),
ei)/ ke（
n),
o(/t' a)/n
(later
o(/tan),
o(/te ke（
n),
pri\n a)/n, o(\s a)/n, o(/s ke（
n),
ta/x' a)/n, ou)k
a)/n, ou)/ ke（
n). This
tendency to combine with some leading word sometimes removes both

a)/n and
ke（
n) to a considerable
distance from the verb to which they properly belong.

§467

Repetition of a)/n and ke（n).

a)/n is not unfrequently repeated in the
same clause, sometimes in order to resume a distant
a)/n, sometimes for rhetorical emphasis,
especially with the negative or equivalent interrogative.
ke（
n) is also
repeated, though rarely, and both
a)/n
and
ke（
n）
are occasionally found in the same clause.

poi/an tin' ou)=n h(/dist' a)\n oi)koi=t'
a)\n po/lin;

[section 14]

## Preface to part II

§pref2

THE first part of this Greek Syntax is a fragment of a larger scheme
of a Greek Series, which was abandoned for good and sufficient
reasons, and it lay in print for some time unpublished. Finally,
yielding to the representations of those who were interested in my
grammatical work, I gave it to the scholarly world as a specimen of
my method of treating a subject about which my studies had revolved
for many years. That method demands repeated reading and repeated
sifting, and the utmost bound of my hope was the continuation of the
general plan by other hands and the acceptance by competent judges
of the results already gained. The framework, as constituted in my
Latin Grammar, which was built on the same lines, has been in use
for a lifetime. The details of my special Greek studies are
accessible in various publications, so that any one who finds aught
worth adopting either in general plan or in individual formula can
readily avail himself of what I have done for the furtherance of
syntactical study.

This second part, like the first, is based on the manuscript of the
original work and the text is with some slight exceptions my own;
but whereas in the first part the contributions of Professor MILLER
were merely supplementary, in the second part, and especially in the
treatment of the article, the collection and the sorting of the
examples have been carried out with his characteristic fulness and
accuracy, so that I desire that all credit be given to him for the
value of this segment of the work as a repository of facts. The
disparateness of the two parts was inevitable under the
circumstances, and the delay occasioned by the coöperation
has been so great that it has seemed best at this point to dissolve
the partnership, maintained for so many years, as it would have been
dissolved at any rate in the natural course of things. An outline of
what remains is in progress, and though it is high time that I cut
down long hope to match the brief space of human life, I do not
hesitate to commit myself to a plan that involves little more than
the shaping of material already in hand.
BASIL L. GILDERSLEEVE, THE JOHNS HOPKINS UNIVERSITY,
BALTIMORE.

Qualification

## Concord of the Predicate

§469

Number.

The common verbal or adjective predicate of two or more subjects is
put in the plural. The substantive predicate follows the rules given
93 and 96.

§470

Singular verb with neuter subjects.

When the combined subjects are neuter, the verb is in the singular,
while the predicate adjective is in the plural.

ta\ me\n ou)=n xwri/a kai\ ta\ de/ndra ou)de/n m'
e)qe/lei dida/skein,

deign to teach me anything.

kai\ to\ e)pi/stasqai xrh=sqai tou/tw|, o(\ a)\n poih=|.
Phaedr. 230 D (see above).

§473

Singular predicate agreeing with nearest or most important
subject.

The common predicate may agree with a singular subject when that
subject is the nearest or the most important. The mobility of the
Greek often makes it hard to decide between 473 and 477.

§481

Adjective predicate with combined subjects of different genders.

When the genders of combined subjects are different, the adjective
predicate takes either the strongest gender or the nearest. In
things with life the masculine gender is the strongest, in things
without life the neuter. In poetry personification has a wide range.

h( me\n *tu/xh kai\ *fi/lippos h)=san tw=n
e)/rgwn ku/rioi,

the issues in their hands, were responsible for the actual
results.

timwme/nou dh\ plou/tou e)n po/lei kai\ tw=n
plousi/wn a)timote/ra a)reth/ te kai\ oi( a)gaqoi/,

When in any state wealth and the wealthy are valued,
virtue and the virtuous are undervalued.

§482

Combined subjects of different persons.

When the persons of combined subjects are different, the first person
is preferred to the second, the second to the third, or else the
person is determined by the nearest subject.

a)ei\ ga\r e)gw\ kai\ o( so\s path\r ... fi/lw
h)=men,

[section 17]

## Qualification of the Subject

## Attributes of the subject

§484

The subject may be qualified by giving it an attribute. An attribute
is that which serves to give a specific character.

§485

Chief Forms of the Attribute

The chief forms of the attribute are:

I. The adjective (including the participle) and its equivalents.

II. Apposition.

§486

Equivalents of the adjective

Equivalents of the adjective are:

1. The Pronouns including the Article.

2. Substantives denoting Rank, Age, Trade.

3. The Genitive Case.

4. The Dative Case.

5. Prepositions with their cases.

6. Adverbs.

7. Relative Clauses.

1. For the Article and the Pronouns, see the sections treating of
these.

§487

Substantives denoting Rank, Age, Trade:

w)= a)/ndres bouleutai/,

XEN. Hell. 2.3.24

have a wider range. Especially common is the use of

## Concord of the adjective attribute.

## The normal rule

§492

The adjective attribute agrees with its substantive in gender,
number, and case.

i(ero\s lo/xos, Sacred Band.

citadel.

## Exceptions.

§493

Exceptions to the above rule are found in the matter of dual concord
and in the employment of the constructio ad sensum
instead of formal agreement.

§494

Plural adjective with dual substantive.

Occasionally a plural adjective is used with a dual substantive, but
not in prose.

§495

du/o with a plural substantive.

From HOMER on
du/o (
du/w) is found with plural substantives as well as with
dual substantives, and the plural is the rule for
du/o (nominative and accusative) in Attic
Prose. Uncommon is the use of
duoi=n
with a plural, [note: See Keck, Ueber
den Dual bei den griechischen Rednern,
Würzburg, 1882, p. 47, and Roeper, De
dualis usu Platonico, Gedani, 1878, p. 25 sq. For a
list of examples, see Ernst Hasse, Der Dualis
im Attischen, Hannover und Leipzig, 1893, p.
6.] and the use of
du/o
(indeclinable) with a substantive in the genitive or the dative
plural is rare in the ATTIC ORATORS and in PLATO. [note: According to Keck, l.c., p. 39, only twice in the
ORATORS:
DEM.41.3 and [42]
DEM., 23; according to Roeper, l.c., p.
20, only twice in PLATO : Legg. 848 A and 908 B (Tim. Locr. 96
A, 97 C, and
Epin. 984 E are
excluded as non Platonic); according to Zander apud
Keck, l.c., the form
du/o is found
with the genitive plural 7 times in THUCYDIDES and 6 times in
XENOPHON, with the dative plural 5 times in THUCYDIDES and once
in XENOPHON.]

§496

Masculine dual forms used generically for the feminine.

The use of the forms
tw/, toi=n, tou/tw,
tou/toin, au)tw/, au)toi=n, tw/de, and the like, with
feminine substantives is no exception to the rule, as these forms
are common to all the genders, though there are also special
feminine forms, which are preferably used in the genitive and
dative. [note: See Ernst Hasse, Der Dualis im Attischen, Hannover und Leipzig,
1893, p. 19.] In like manner, the masculine dual forms of
descriptive adjectives and of participles are sometimes used
generically for the feminine. [note: See Keck, p. 37
infra and p. 38 supra; also Hasse,
l.c., pp. 26.36, and 37, where, however, no instance of a
feminine simple adjective in
oin or
of a feminine participle in
oin is
cited.]

e)rei/sat', w)= pai=, pleuro\n a)mfide/cion

e)mfu/nte [note: (sc. Antigone and Ismene)] tw=| fu/santi,

SOPH. O. C. 1112-3
.
a)qana/twn meta\ fu=lon i)/ton
prolipo/nt' a)nqrw/pous |
*ai)dw\s kai\ *ne/mesis, HES. O. et D. 199-200.

§497

Agreement according to sense.

There is no recorded exception in prose. In poetry there are
occasional exceptions, the real gender, and not the grammatical,
being regarded.

w)= xai=re, kollikofa/ge *boiwti/dion,
AR. Ach. 872.

AR. Ach. 872 (see above).

AESCHYL. Cho. 893:

te/qnhkas, fi/ltat' *ai)gi/sqou bi/a.

fi/le te/knon. [note: For 11.244-5, see 503.]

§498

Variations in participial concord.

For deviations in the concord of the participle, which in its
ordinary use differs from the adjective in that it conveys the
notion of predication, see 122-4 and other sections under
Agreement of the Predicate with the Subject.

§499

Deviations in concord of pronouns.

Pronouns that have different forms for different genders and numbers,
agree in gender and number with the substantives they represent. But
there are frequent variations according to sense, the name of a
town, for example, suggesting the inhabitants, or a plural pronoun
being used of a generic singular (sometimes the reverse).

§500

Agreement with a synonym.

The tendency of the Greek language to sudden changes of subject
without the mention by name of the new subject (compare 71), is
largely responsible for the few instances that are cited of the
agreement of a pronoun with the gender of some synonymous word,
instead of with the natural or the grammatical gender of the word
that the pronoun represents.

§501

Neuter pronouns referring to masculine or feminine substantives.

Not an exception to the rule of concord is the case of a neuter
pronoun referring to a masculine or feminine substantive regarded as
a thing. Cf. 126. In most of the instances cited, however, the
reference is rather to some thought implied in the substantive than
to the substantive itself.

§502

Plural of neuter pronouns for the singular.

Neuter pronouns are often used in the plural where we might expect
the singular. [note: For Neuter Plural of Adjectives
instead of Singular, see 37.]

§503

Agreement of common adjective attribute.

The common adjective attribute of two or more substantives agrees
with the nearest. Exceptions are explicable.

si=tos polu\s kai\ pro/bata, XEN. An.

§504

Repetition of the common attribute.

The common attribute may be repeated for emphasis or rhetorical
balance. So with
kai\ ... kai/, me\n ...
de/.

w)/|eto kth/sesqai e)k tou/twn o)/noma me/ga kai\
du/namin me ga/lhn, XEN. An. 2.6.17; He thought
that he should acquire from this a great name and great power.

## Position of the adjective attribute.

§505

The adjective attribute precedes the substantive. This, which is the
original position, so often yields to rhythmical and rhetorical
considerations that a sharp difference between the order, attribute
and substantive, and the order, substantive and attribute, cannot be
formulated.

mikra=s zhmi/as a)ci/ous

; Deserving [only] a SLIGHT penalty.

oi)kono/mos deinh/,

(but) ONE body and ONE
soul (504, chiasm).

§506

Separation of attribute from substantive.

The attribute may be separated from its substantive by one or more
words.

Removal often increases the emphasis, the mind being thus forced to
recall or await the substantive. So numerals are often put at a
distance as if for the convenience of adding. The interval is seldom
excessive in prose. In poetry, on the other hand, it is sometimes
very considerable, and sometimes the conception seems rather
predicative than attributive, but the rhythm aids in bridging the
chasm. The beginnings of verses are really near. So PIND. O. 6.1-2,
cited below. But compare P. 9.5-6, O. 11.19-21 (both cited below),
and especially O. 12.5-6, and see the editor's notes on those
passages.

pleone/kthm' a)/ndres *)aqhnai=oi me/g' u(ph=rcen
*fili/ppw|,

with.

§508

Position of common attribute.

When the attribute belongs to two or more words, it is generally put
before them all, sometimes after them all, sometimes after the
first, occasionally after one of the intermediate substantives.

pollai\ kriqai\ kai\ puroi/, XEN. An.

## Peculiar forms of the adjective attribute

§513

The demonstrative and other adjective pronouns present important
peculiarities. Of these pronouns the most common is the article.

[section 19]

## The Article

§514

The function of the article, which was originally a demonstrative,
and always has more or less demonstrative force, is to fix a
floating adjective or substantive. In the earlier stages of the
language
i(/ppos means
‘the horse’ (literally
‘the swift one’） [note: But cf. A.
J. P. xxiii (1902), 22. Oertel, Study of Language, p.
306.] as well as ‘a horse,’
and we have an implicit article as well as an explicit article
(
o(, h(, to/). This implicit article
served at first as particular or generic. Afterwards the particular
use required the reinforcement of the explicit article, but in the
generic use the employment of the explicit article was optional.
Particular: (
a)/nqrwpos),
o( a)/nqrwpos, the man;
generic:
a)/nqrwpos, o( a)/nqrwpos,
man.

Proper names being in their nature particular do not require the
explicit article, and when the article is used with them, it retains
much of its original demonstrative force.

It is impossible to draw a sharp line of demarcation between the uses
of the attributive article at different stages. The Homeric
demonstrative use passes over into the articular use, and the Attic
articular use approaches the Homeric demonstrative use. In the one
case we have the germ of a new life; in the other, the survival of
the old. To the Attic the Homeric
o(
ge/rwn could hardly have been distinguishable from the
everyday
o( ge/rwn of prose. Much
depends on the position in the verse. Sometimes there may be
apposition, sometimes the article may be used for contrast. When
Pindar said
a)/riston me\n u(/dwr, o( de\
xruso/s, the verse brings out the pause, the feeling of
antithesis. We say that there is no articular infinitive in Homer,
because in the only apparent example, Od. 20.52:
a)ni/h kai\ to\ fula/ssein, the appositional
explanation brings the passage in line with the use elsewhere (Od.
1.370; 11.358-9) and with the later use of
tou=to, which is the demonstrative article
‘writ large’ (
PLATO,
Gorg. 449C).

In the older language and in the higher poetry that follows the older
norm the article proper is not so much used as in Attic prose and as
in comic poetry, which approaches the language of everyday life.
Homer gives the norm, which is followed closely by Pindar. The lyric
parts of tragedy are more anarthrous than the dialogue. The dialogue
of comedy differs decidedly from that of tragedy in this respect.
Old phrases, especially prepositional phrases, retain in the heyday
of Attic their anarthrous forms. Dialects vary, and the Doric is
said to have affected the hearty homely article. See the
Lacedaemonian chorus in the Lysistrate. Authors vary, and whereas
some eschew the article with proper names or use it gingerly,
others, like Plato , are exceedingly free with it. On the other
hand, the familiar use of the article has led to exactness and
finesse, and the subtle variations in the employment of it add a
special charm to Attic prose. [note: Cf. A. J. P. xxiii
(1902), 121.]

The substantive use of the article is in Attic prose a survival, and
is found chiefly in fixed phrases and certain old-fashioned turns of
expression.

## Substantive Use

§515

o(\ me\n . . . o(\ de/, o(\ me\n . . . de/,
me\n . . . o(\ de/.

Very common is
o(\ me\n . . . o(\ de/,
now used with definite reference to persons or things mentioned,
the one . . . the other, the former . . . the latter, the
latter . . . the former, now used indefinitely simply in
contrast to each other. One or the other (more commonly the latter)
of the two articles may also be replaced by a substantive or a
pronoun.

Here belong also the adverbial uses of the article

§518

o(\ de/ without reference to
o(\ me/n

o(\ de/ may be used by itself without
reference to an expressed or an implied
o(\
me/n, rarely, however, in Attic of the subject of the
preceding sentence. Especially common in Plato is
to\ de/, which is often unnecessarily
treated as if it were an adverbial phrase, as for that matter,
whereas. (C. W. E. M., JAPA., xxxix, 121
ff.)

o(\ de/
not same as subject of preceding sentence:

kai\ e)gw\ th\n gunai=ka a)pie/nai
e)ke/leuon . . . h(\ de\ . . . ou)k h)/qelen,

; And I bade my wife go away but she would not
(refused).

§521

kai\ to/n, kai\ th/n.

In Attic prose
kai\ to/n, kai\ th/n are
used in oratio obliqua where
kai\
o(/s, kai\ h(/ (see 522) would be used in oratio
recta. In poetry and in HERODOTUS, the use is not limited
to oratio obliqua.

e)/fh ga\r . . . e)re/sqai au)to\n o()poi i)/oi .
. . kai\ to\n ei)pei=n o(/ti e)pi\ dei=pnon,
PLATO, Conv. 174A; He said that
he asked him whither he was going and he made answer, To a
banquet.

§522

o(/s, h(/, o(/ used like the
substantive article.

The use of
o(/s, h(/, o(/ as a
demonstrative is almost confined in Attic prose to the combinations

kai\ o(/s, kai\ h(/, kai\ oi(/, and
the phrases
h)= d' o(/s, h)= d' h(/. In
poetry the use is more free.

kai\o(\s h(gei=to, XEN. An. 6.5.22;
And he was in the van.

§528

Substantive article unsupported by a particle.

HOMER uses the article as a substantive demonstrative with more
freedom than the

a)lla\ ta\ me\n poli/wn e)\c e)pra/qomen, ta\
de/dastai, HOM. Il. 1.125.

§529

Article as antecedent of the relative.

Wherever the article is used as a full demonstrative, it may be used
as the antecedent of the relative, but the regular antecedent of the
relative is
ou(=tos. In Attic prose the
few examples cited are mainly in the neuter gender, and may be
explained and have been explained on the same principle as the
ordinary article with any word or group of words.

§530

Article as a relative.

Out of the demonstrative use grows the relative use, for which see
Relative Sentence. The ancient grammarians called
the demonstrative article
a)/rqron
protaktiko/n, the relative
a)/rqron
u(potaktiko/n, and relative and article were blended in
the consciousness of the people.

## Adjective use

§531

The article is commonly treated under two heads, that of the
individual or specific article and that of the generic article.

## Individual or Specific Article

§532

The use of the Individual or Specific Article is so much like the
English that only divergencies need be noticed at length.

§533

Article with objects present to the mind.

Like the English the Attic article is used of objects that are
present to the mind or senses, well-known, notorious, expected,
recurring, customary — the Anaphoric Article or Article of
Reference.

oi( ma/rtures memarturh/kasin,
ANDOC.1.25; The witnesses have
testified.

o( . . . a)/nqrwpos e)/ndon h)=n,

Lys. 1.11
; The fellow was within.

o( po/lemos e(rpe/tw, Ar. Lys. 129 and
130; On with the war.

sta/seis e)n tai=s po/lesin . . .
e)gi/gnonto,
THUC.1.12.2;
Factions kept breaking out in the
(different) cities.

qa/natos . . . h( zhmi/a,
DEM.20.167; The
(regular) penalty is death.

diekwlu/qhsan de\ u(po\ tou= genome/nou
seismou=,

They were prevented by the occurrence of the
well-known (great) earthquake.

*ce/rchs o(/te e)k th=s *(ella/dos h(tthqei\s
th=| ma/xh| [after having been defeated in
the (famous) battle]
a)pexw/rei, XEN. An. 1.2.9.

§534

Possessive article.

The article is used very freely in prose, in orators, philosophers,
historians alike, to intimate possession. So especially in family
relations, parts of the body, personal belongings. In elevated
poetry this possessive article is very little used. The language is
content with the anarthrous substantive. But as possession is more
freely emphasized in poetry than in prose, the possessive pronoun
and possessive genitive are often employed where prose would be
satisfied with the article. (See Possessive Pronouns.）
Comedy admits the prose use without difficulty.

th\n klei=n sunetri/bhn kai\ th\n kefalh\n
katea/ghn,
AND.1.61;
My collar-bone was crushed and my head broken.

§535

Article with numbers.

The Greek language uses the article with numerals more freely than
the English, as, for instance, of fractions, ro[udot ]nd numbers,
accepted standards, and the like.

§536

The article with proper names.

Proper Names for the most part do not require the article. The
conditions under which the article is used are set forth in the
following sections. In connection with these, see also
Blass,
Rhein. Mus. 44 (1889), pp. 6-17; Carolus Schmidt, De
articulo in nominibus propriis apud Atticos scriptores pedestres,
Kiliae, 1890; H. Kallenberg, Philologus 49 (1890), pp. 515-47;
id., Studien über den gr. Artikel, Berlin,
1891, Pr.; B. L. Gildersleeve, A. J. P. xi (1890), 483-7; Uckermann,
Über den Artikel bei Eigennamen in den Komödien
des Aristophanes, Berlin, 1892, Pr.; Zucker, Beobachtungen über den Gebrauch des Artikels bei
Personennamen in Xenophons Anabasis, Nürnberg,
1899; A. J. P. xxiv (1903), 482; Völker, Syntax der
griechischen Papyri: I. Der Artikel, Münster, 1903, Pr.

§537

Article with names of persons.

The Article with the Names of Persons belongs to popular diction, and
may be considered a norm of familiar style. It is not found in epic
poetry, the examples usually cited being demonstrative rather than
articular. It is rare in the higher lyric, more common in comedy
than in tragedy. In prose some authors use it steadily for anaphora
or for contrast. Plato uses it freely. The orators vary. With them
the rule is not to use the article with the name of the opponent,
who, as is natural, is often mentioned.

Names of opponents anarthrous (Schmidt, l.c.). 1.58:

zhth=sai th\n boulh/n . . . , a)pe/fhnen h( boulh/ . . .

§538

National appellatives, etc.

Names of nations, inhabitants of cities, etc., as such do not require
the article.
*(/ellhnes, however, in the
course of time came to be viewed as an adjective, in contrast with

ba/rbaroi, and regularly took the
article, but in Herodotus it is treated like any other national
appellative.

§539

Article with appositive proper names.

In combination with appositive proper names, the article is used to
identify by demonstration (resumption).

§540

Appositive proper name without article.

The omission of the article is the regular thing in formal and
official style. Outside of this sphere,
a)nh/r with a proper adjective is a frequent substitute for
the anarthrous proper adjective used substantively.

§541

Article with names of gods.

Names of gods as such do not require the article, but the articular
form is very common, especially in familiar language. When the name
of a god in the genitive depends upon an articular substantive it is
generally accompanied by the article, but if the governing
substantive is without the article, the name of the god is
anarthrous too.

§542

Names of gods in oaths.

In oaths the article is required except with the name of Zeus. With
the name of Zeus familiarity has brought about the fading of the
oath, and the article is usually omitted.

§543

Use of article in complex of name of god and epithet.

When an epithet is added to the name of a god, both take the article
or neither (fixity of religious phrases the world over). [note: For the usage of the ISS, see Meisterhans3,
§ 86.3 and 4.]

tou= *dio\s tou= *)olumpi/ou,

§547

Article with names of countries.

Adjectival names of countries and of urban territories require the
article, but names of countries in
-i/a
that are identical with the feminines of related adjectives
fluctuate.

The general exceptions hold good as to the predicate (666),
enumerations (568), and the common article (603).

moi=ra.

Herodotus. Such proper names are frankly adjectival and
gh= and

often retain the full sense of “soil.”

§549

*)asi/a, *eu)rw/ph, *libu/h.

Of the grand divisions of the earth,
*)asi/a, which is never adjectival in prose, and
*eu)rw/ph, which is everywhere a
substantive, require the article, whilst
*libu/h varies. The rule is subject to the same exceptions
as in 547.

h)=lqen e)k th=s *)asi/as,
THUC.1.9.2; He came from Asia.

a)nexw/rhsan e)k th=s *eu)rw/phs, Ibid.
1.89.2; They retreated from Europe.

a)fikome/nous a)po\ th=s *libu/hs, Ibid.
7.50.1; Having arrived from Libya, but immediately
thereafter, Ibid. 2:
a)penexqe/ntes ga\r e)s
*libu/hn, Carried out of their course to Libya.

e)n tw=| metacu\ *)asi/hs te kai\
*libu/hs,
HDT.2.16; In the
space between Asia and Libya (enumeration).
h)\ *)asi/h| h)\ *eu)rw/ph|, Ibid. 4.198;
To either Asia or Europe.

th=s ga\r gh=s a(pa/shs . . . di/xa tetmhme/nhs,
kai\ th=s me\n *)asi/as, th=s d' *eu)rw/phs kaloume/nhs,

ISOC.4.179; The whole earth
being divided into two parts, the one called Asia, the other
Europe (article omitted because
*)asi/as and
*eu)rw/phs are
predicates).

Hellenica alone
*)asi/a, according to
Keller's index, occurs 26 times. The article is used each time.

(partitive gen. in predication).

divisions are named together [Kallenberg, Philol. 46 (1887),
729 = 49 (1890), 525]. 2.16:

§551

*eu)rw/ph:

ORATORES ATTICI. The rule is always followed as, e.g.
ISOC.4.68:

kaq' o(\n (sc.
xro/non)
e(ka/teroi th=s *eu)rw/phs
e)ph=rxon. 117. 187. Elsew.

PLATO , Alc. II, 141 B:
pa/shs *eu)rw/phs
(exceptional).
Criti.112E
(enumeration, see 550). Gorg. 523 E (see 550). 524 A (see 550).
Menex. 239 D (see 550). Tim. 24 E (enumeration, see 550). 25 B:

*libu/hs (no article)
me\n h)=rxon me/xri pro\s *ai)/gupton, th=s de\
*eu)rw/phs me/xri *turrhni/as.

XEN. The only exception to the rule is An. 7.8, [25]:
tw=n e)n *eu)rw/ph| *qra|kw=n (Kallenberg,
l.c., p. 525), and this passage is spurious.
*eu)rw/ph is not nearly so common as
*)asi/a, occurring only four times (see
Keller's index) in the Hellenica, viz., 3.2.9. 4.2.6. 3.15. 8.5.

THUC.1.89.2:

e)peidh\ *mh=doi a)nexw/rhsan e)k th=s
*eu)rw/phs. 2.97.5. 6. All three exx. follow the rule.

In 2.26 and 2.33, all Mss., and in 4.49 ABC, omit the article in the
phrase
dia\ pa/shs th=s *eu)rw/phs. In
3.115:
ta\ e)pe/keina th=s *eu)rw/-

phs, only R has the article. In all the
passages the article should be read unless, as is likely,

§553

Article with chorographic genitive and partitive genitive of name
of country.

The Chorographic Genitive (cf. “Bethlehem of
Judaea”) generally takes the article and so does the
Postpositive Partitive Genitive of the name of a country.

§554

sometimes show a tendency to resist the article.

§555

Article with names of towns.

Names of Towns do not require the article, and even the anaphoric use
is sometimes due merely to the desire for the avoidance of hiatus.

ei)/xet' a)\n *)amfi/polin,
DEM.1.8 and similarly very often; but

tw=| th\n *)Amfi/polin fa/skein
paradw/sein, Ibid. 2.6 (article used to avoid hiatus).

*eu)/boian de\ kai\ to\n *)wrwpo\n a)nt'
*)amfipo/lews u(mi=n a)podw/sei, Ibid. 6.30 (art. with

*)w. to avoid hiatus).

§556

Addition of po/lis in herodotus.

The word
po/lis may be added by way of
explanation. It is omitted with well-known cities, with names
preceded by the article, in attributive prepositional phrases, and
sometimes when the same name is repeated in close proximity. When

po/lis has been used with the first
of two or more coördinate names, it need not be repeated
with the succeeding ones.

e)s *boutou=n po/lin,

a)naple/onti, Ibid. 97 (well-known cities).

§558

Seas.

*po/ntos always has the article even when
it designates the country along the Black Sea. When
*eu)/ceinos is expressed, the forms are

o( *eu)/ceinos to/ntos and
o( po/ntos o( *eu)/ceinos. *maiw=tis and

*proponti/s always take the article,
the former being accompained by the word
li/mnh. For the Aegaean, the Greeks said
to\ *ai)gai=on pe/lagos, o( *ai)gai=os
po/ntos, and
to\ *ai)gai=on. o(
*)adri/as and
h( *su/rtis
are the rule. Other names of seas are adjectival; hence regularly

h( *)eruqra\ qa/latta, h( *kaspi/h
qa/lassa (HDT. ), etc.
*(ellh/|spontos prefers the article, which, however, is
frequently omitted in prepositional phrases.
*)wkeano/s and
*bo/sporos
seem to require the article, but
*bo/sporos = Panticapaeum follows the usage of towns.

§559

Omission and use of article and potamo/s with names of rivers.

I. As a general rule when the name of a river is mentioned for the
first time, the type
o( *nei=los is used
of well-known rivers, the type
o( *strumw\n
potamo/s of fairly well-known rivers, and the type

*bro/ggos potamo/s or
potamo\s *bro/ggos of obscure rivers.

tou= *nei/lou,
HDT.2.10.

oi(\ de\ . . . e)/frazon w(s . . . ei)/h . . .
h( *paioni/h e)pi\ tw=| *strumo/ni potamw=|
pepolisme/nh
HDT., 5.13.

e)c *)illuriw=n de\ r(e/wn . . . *)/aggros
potamo\s e)sba/llei . . . e)s potamo\n *bro/ggon
HDT., 4.49.

II. When a river that has once been mentioned is referred to again by
name in the same context, the article is used with the second name,
but to this in the case of obscure rivers the word
potamo/s is generally attached.

kata\ to\n *nei=lon,
HDT.2.10 follows
tou=
*nei/lou mentioned a few lines preceding.
e)pi\ tw=| *strumo/ni potamw=| . . . o( de\
*strumw/n,
HDT.5.13.

e)s potamo\n *bro/ggon, o: de\
*bro/ggos,
HDT.4.49.

kath/kontes e)pi\ potamo\n tw=| ou)/noma ke/etai
*pantika/phs,
HDT.4.18; but

diaba/nti to\n *pantika/phn
potamo/n, Ibid. 19.

III. All rivers are anarthrous and are sometimes accompanied by the
word
potamo/s

I. In the predicate.

to\n de\ dh\ potamo\n tou=ton . . . suneba/lleto
ei)=nai *nei=lon,
HDT.2.33.

2. In enumerations.

a)/lloi mega/loi . . . e)sba/llousi e)s au)to/n,
*)/atlas kai\ *au)/ras kai\ *ti/bisis. dia\ de\ *qrhi/khs . . .
r(e/ontes *)/aqrus kai\ *no/hs kai\ *)arta/nhs e)kdidou=si e)s
to\n *)/istron,
HDT.4.49.

3. In dependence upon the word
pedi/on.

e)n *maia/ndrou pedi/w|,
HDT.1.18.

4. In attributive prepositional phrases; with improper prepositions;
in the phrase
a)po\ . . . e)s, e)pi/,
me/xri; and in double designations of locality.

th=| e)pi\ *qermw/donti ma/xh|,
HDT.4.110.

me/xri *)/istrou potamou=,
HDT.4.89.

a)po\ ga\r *)/istrou e)pi\ *borusqe/nea,

HDT.4.101.

au)tou= peri\ *)/acion potamo\n kai\ po/lin
*qe/rmhn,
HDT.7.124（
*)/acios already mentioned in 123).

5. When the name is emphatically placed at the head of the sentence.

*nei/lou me/n nun pe/ri tosau=ta
ei)rh/sqw,
HDT.2.35.

6. When an articular attributive expression is attached to the name.

*nei=lo/s te o( *ai)gu/ptios potamo\s . . . kai\
*fa=sis o( *ko/lxos,
HDT.4.45.

plh\n *nei/lou tou= *ai)gupti/ou, Ibid.
53. [note: For further details, see Hermann
Kallenberg, Studien über den griechischen Artikel,
Berlin, 1891.]

DEM. [59]
DEM., 99:

o( ga\r *)aswpo\s potamo\s me/gas

In the Anabasis
potamo/s is regularly
added to the articular name, and even the most obscure rivers often
take the article, but the following rule is observed: In introducing
a comparatively unknown river, Xenophon uses the article when
narrating the march of his own army, but omits it in merely
geographical descriptions. 1.2.11:
ei)s
*kau+/strou pedi/on. 1.4.1: e)celau/nei . . . e)pi\ to\n
*pu/ramon potamo/n. 2.5.1: a)fiknou=ntai e)pi\ to\n *zapa/tan
potamo/n. 5.3.8: e)/tuxe de\ diarre/wn . . . potamo\s
*selinou=s. kai\ e)n *)efe/sw| de\ . . . *selinou=s potamo\s
pararrei=. 5.6. 36: plei=n ei)s *fa=sin and so regularly
because the name of the city is included. 7.8.8:
ei)s *kai:/kou pedi/on.

In the Hellenica the articular name without
potamo/s is more commonly used, the rivers of the
Hellenica being for the most part well known. So the Alpheus,
Eurotas, Attic and Boeotian Cephisus, and the Maeander. In the case
of the Eurymedon, the Pactolus, and the Cladaus, the articular name
is followed by
potamo/s. See Kallenberg,
l.c., p. 11.

THUCYDIDES in general follows the same rules as Herodotus, but for
obvious reasons the anarthrous name with
potamo/s is much less common than in Herodotus. The
names of insignificant rivers of Sicily and Lower Italy, which had
become familiar to the Athenians as a result of the Sicilian
expedition, follow the type
o( *)axelw=|os
potamo/s. 1.46.4: dia\ de\ th=s *qesprwti/dos *)axe/rwn potamo\s
r(e/wn . . . r(ei= de\ kai\ *qu/amis potamo/s. 98.1: *)hio/na
th\n e)pi\ *strumo/ni. 100.1: h( e)p' *eu)rume/donti potamw=| .
. . pezomaxi/a. 100.3: e)pi\ de\ *strumo/na (omission of
the article exceptional)
pe/myantes . . .
oi)kh/toras. 2.5.2: o( ga\r *)aswpo\s potamo/s. 96.3: me/xri . .
. tou= *strumo/nos potamou= (but 2.97.2. 4.102.4. 108.1.
6. 5.7.4. 7.9, with the article alone). 2.96.4:
me/xri tou= *)oski/ou potamou=. (The Oscius had become
so familiar to Thucydides that he unwittingly presupposes general
acquaintance with the river on the part of the Athenians.) 97.1:

me/xri *)/istrou potamou= . . . e)c
*)abdh/rwn e)s *)/istron. 99.4: pe/ran *)aci/ou me/xri
*strumo/nos. 102.2: o( ga\r *)axelw=|os potamo/s (but
ibid. 3. 3.7.4. 106.1 without
potamo/s).
3.19.2:
dia\ tou= *maia/ndrou pedi/ou. 6.4.1:
u(pe\r *pantaku/ou . . . potamou=. 7.80.5: o(/pws e)peidh\

§560

Omission and use of article and o)/ros with names of mountains.

The use of the names of mountains follows in general the same rules
as prevail in the case of names of rivers. But between the article
and the word
o)/ros, the name of a
mountain is found only when it is distinctly adjectival, and
ordinary apposition, which occurs rarely, if at all, in classic
authors in the case of rivers, cannot be questioned with names of
mountains.

§562

Article with other proper names.

SHRINES and SACRED INCLOSURES, being substantivized adjectives, have
the article, but the FESTIVALS of the gods fluctuate as do also the
names of the MONTHS whether accompanied by
mh/n or not. The names of the TRIBES vary. The Greek says
of SHIPS
h( *)argw/, h( *salamini/a, h(
*pa/ralos, just as we say “The
Etruria,” “The Umbria”; and the
article is used with STATUES as in English.

## Generic article.

§563

The principle of the generic article is the selection of a
representative or normal individual. So in English to a certain
extent: “He played the villain,”
“acted the simpleton,”
“was not the gentleman”;
“the horse,”
“the cow,”
“the ox,” and the animals
generally. The plural is foreign to our idiom.

Of this article little use is made in the highest range of Greek
poetry, and almost all the examples have been, or can be, explained
on other principles, sometimes on the principle of resumption
(anaphora), sometimes on the principle of contrast.

ou) mo/non a)/r' . . . o( ge/rwn di\s pai=s
gi/gnoit' a)/n, a)lla\ kai\ o( mequsqei/s,

PLATO, Legg. 646A
; It would appear, then, that not only the old man
becomes a child the second time, but also the drunken man.

di\s paides oi( ge/rontes,

; Old men are twice children.

ponhro\n . . . ponhro\n o( sukofa/nths,

; A mischievous thing, a mischievous thing, is the
informer.

§565

Abstract Nouns with the Article:

Under this head the influeńce of the original
personification is to be considered, and the article with the
abstract is almost on a line with the articular names of the gods
(A. J. P. xi, 53), the
h( a)reth/ of
EURIPIDES (Suppl. 596) with the
h(
*)adra/steia of AESCHYLUS (
P. V.
936). In fact it is often doubtful whether we have a
personification or an abstract noun, especially in the nominative.

§566

Abstracts without the Article:

Everywhere, as in English, from HOMER to DEMOSTHENES, and even in the
subtle language of philosophy differences that have been set up by
grammarians are often imaginary.

§567

Difference between articular and anarthrous abstract nouns.

That no vital difference was felt is shown by the easy passage from
the articular to the non-articular (anarthrous) form, and by the
occasional combination of the anarthrous abstract noun with the
articular infinitive, the article simply serving to give the
infinitive a case-prefix.

## Absence of the article.

§568

Prepositional phrases and other formulae may dispense with the
article as in the earlier language. So also proverbs, enumerations,
the ordinal in expressions of time, and
basileu/s of the Persian king. Compare wife and
child, out of sight, out of mind, out of window, in harbor, to
town; sun, moon, stars. But anaphora or contrast may
bring back the article at any time and there is no pedantical
uniformity.

To prove the variations in detail would be impossible here and
unprofitable. Nor is it necessary to cite examples from poetry,
which fall under the rule already given.

ei)s a)/stu,

LYS.12.16
; To town (but

ei)s to\ a)/stu,

Ibid. 54
).

order),]

§572

Use of basileu/s

basileu/s for King of Persia is the
official name of the great King. So always in ISS., but authors vary
as in the case of proper names, with which
basileu/s is to be classed. The orators have
basileu/s, but also
basileu\s o( me/gas, o( me/gas basileu/s, o( *persw=n
basileu/s.

para\ tou= *persw=n basile/ws. 32: pro\s to\n
*persw=n basile/a.

basile/a.

## The article with substantivized adjectives and participles.

§573

Adjectives and participles when used as substantives regularly take
the article in prose. The omission of the article is governed by the
same principles as in the case of substantives. For examples and
other details, see 28.29.30.31.36.39, and 40.

§574

Attraction of articular participle or adjective.

The articular participle or adjective is commonly attracted into the
gender of its predicate.

ou)/te plh=qo/s e)stin ou)/te i)sxu\s h( e)n tw=|
pole/mw| ta\s vi/kas poiou=sa, XEN. An. 3.1.42; It
is neither numbers nor strength that win victories in war.

nou=s e)stin o( diakosmw=n te kai\ pa/ntwn
ai)/tios, PLATO , Phaedo, 97 C; Mind is the
regulative principle and the cause of all things.

§575

Article with adverbs and prepositional and other expressions used
as substantives.

phrases, and other expressions.

.] the person alone.

§580

Masculine or feminine article with the genitive case.

The masculine or feminine article with the genitive merely shows
connexion.
o( is commonly
son, and
h( commonly
daughter, but the precise relation is to be
determined from the context. The construction is not used in
official documents except in the genitive case, in which the article
is obligatory.

§581

Neuter article with the genitive case.

The neuter article, singular and plural, with the genitive has a
varied translation. But the article often serves merely to give
especial emphasis, often of dislike, like our English
‘this thing of,’ and may be left untranslated.

§582

The article with the infinitive.

The article is used with the infinitive outside of epic poetry
proper. This enables the infinitive to assume all the constructions
of the substantive that are not inconsistent with its verbal nature.

Inconsistent with the character of the infinitive as a verb would be
the sacrifice of its subject or object.

h( timh\ th=s po/lews becomes
to\ tima=sqai th\n po/lin,
DEM.18.93.

h( swthri/a th=s nh/sou becomes
to\ sw=sai th\n nh=son,
DEM.18.100.

h( tou= belti/stou a)/gnoia kai\ to\ a)gnoei=n
to\ be/ltiston, PLATO , Alcib. II, 143 E.

For details of the use of the article with the infinitive, see
Articular Infinitive.

## Article in combination with pronouns.

§583

The article is used with sundry pronouns, partly coinciding with our
idiom, partly differing from it.

§584

Article with personal and reflexive pronouns.

Noteworthy is the occasional use, in familiar language, of the
article with the personal and the reflexive pronoun.

§585

Article with interrogatives.

The article with substantive and adjective interrogatives is found
chiefly in dramatic poetry and in dialogue. So we sometimes
colloquially say “the what?”

§586

Article with toiou=tos and the
like.

toiou=tos, toio/sde, thlikou=tos, thliko/sde,
tosou=tos, toso/sde, are treated like ordinary
adjectives, and, like them, may be used substantively with the
article, or adjectively in the attributive position, but
tosou=tos and
toso/sde in either use are rare.

§593

o( e(/teros (e(/teros) in a bad sense.

o( e(/teros (
e(/teros) is sometimes used in the sense of bad,
hostile. Familiar is the phrase
ple/on qa/teron poiei=n, to make bad
worse. Cf. the English “to laugh on the other side
of the mouth.”

§594

a)/llos, o( a)/llos.

a)/llos means another
(a different one);
o(
a)/llos, the other, the rest. So
oi( a)/lloi.

sunhkolou/qhsan de\ . . . kai\ tw=n a)/llwn
stratiwtw=n w(s diako/sioi, XEN. An. 2.5.30; There
followed also of the other soldiers about two hundred.

sunhkolou/qoun de\ kai\ a)/lloi *persw=n . . .
ei)s triakosi/ous, XEN. An. 2.5.35; There followed
also other (s of the) Persians to the
number of three hundred.

h)=san de\ kai\ a)/llai kw=mai pollai\ . . . e)n
tou/tw| tw=| pedi/w|, XEN. An. 3.5.1; There were
also many other villages in the plain.

other cases and with adverbial forms.

§599

a)/llos and e(/teros with appositive force.

Both
a)/llos and (more rarely)
e(/teros have an appositive force, which
the corresponding words lack in English. The rendering is generally
“too,”
“besides,” but sometimes the
translation of the contrast would be too cumbrous.

ou) ga\r h)=n xo/rtos ou)de\ a(/llo ou)de\n
de/ndron, XEN. An. 1.5.5; There was no grass nor
any tree besides.

h(gei=to d' au)tai=s (= the
Lacedaemonian ships)
*tamw\s . . . e)/xwn nau=s
e(te/ras *ku/rou pe/nte kai\ ei)/kosin (with
twenty-five additional ships that belonged to Cyrus, not
twenty-five others of Cyrus's), XEN. An. 1.4.2.

sxedo\n a(pa/shs ai)ti/an ou)=san. 5.148: kai\ ga\r e)kei/nwn
ma=llon a)/gantai th\n h(=ttan th\n e)n *qermopu/lais h)\ ta\s
a)/llas ni/kas. 6.16. 10.66.

## Position of the article.

§602

The article always precedes in prose. In Homer the article,
accompanied by a relative clause, rarely follows in the sense of the
reduplicated article
ou(=tos.

§603

Repetition and non-repetition of the article.

The article may be common to a number of copulated substantives, even
when they are of different genders or numbers, or it may be repeated
with each member. Theoretically the repetition compels a separate
consideration while the omission suggests unity. Practically the
Greeks were almost as loose as we are prone to be, and a sharp
difference cannot be made. [note: See A. J. P. vi
(1885), 486.]

Thecretically then:

oi( . . . strathgoi\ kai\ oi( loxagoi/,
XEN. An. 3.5.14, the generals and the captains (as
different classes of officers), but
oi(
strathgoi\ kai\ loxagoi/ (cf. ibid. 1.7.2), the
generals and captains (officers as distinguished from the
privates).

Practically:

to\ o(/moion kai\ to\ a)no/moion kai\ to\
tau)to\n kai\ e(/teron,
PLATO, Theaet. 186A; The like and the unlike and
the identical and different.

oi( au)toi\ o(/rkoi kai\ cummaxi/a,

THUC.1.102.4.

§606

Article with appositions of personal pronouns.

Appositions with the personal pronoun take the article, even when the
pronoun is involved in the verb. Omission occurs in poetry, rarely
in prose. Compare also 13.

## The Article in the Combination of Article, Adjective, and Substantive

§608

In the combination of article, adjective, and substantive five
positions are to be noticed. Three of these are attributive and two
predicative.

§609

Attributive positions.

There are three attributive positions. The first,
o( a)gaqo\s a)nh/r, is the most simple, natural, and
straightforward, and is briefer (
su/ntomos) than the second,
o( a)nh\r
o( a)gaqo/s, which is more deliberate, and somewhat more
rhetorical, pompous (
o)gkw/dhs),
passionate. [note: ARISTOT. Rhet. 3.6 (1407 b,
26-37). The examples given are
th=s
h(mete/ras gunaiko/s for
suntomi/a and
th=s gunaiko\s
th=s h(mete/ras for
o)/gkos. Cf. A. J. P. xx (1899), 459.] The third
position,
a)nh\r o( a)gaqo/s, is the
epanorthotic, self-corrective, or slipshod position. In prose, it
produces an effect of familiar ease. In poetry, as the substantive
is more frequently anarthrous, the difference between
a)nh\r o( a)gaqo/s and
o( a)nh\r o( a)gaqo/s cannot be insisted on. [note: A. J. P. xxiii (1902), 8.]

oi( a)/grioi oi)=es kai\ oi( o)/noi oi( a)/grioi
a)sinei=s ei)sin, XEN. Cyr. 1.4.7; The wild sheep
and the wild asses are harmless.

oi)kh/sete de\ ta\s au)ta\s oi)ki/as kai\ xw/ran
th\n au)th\n e)rga/sesqe kai\ gunaici\ tai=s au)tai=s
sunoikh/sete, XEN. Cyr. 4.4.10; You will inhabit
the same houses and till the same land and live with the same
wives (as for land, you will till the same, as for
wives, you will live with the same).

ORATORES ATTICI. In the case of the adjective possessive pronoun,
according to statistics furnished by Dr. Alfred W. Milden, the first
attributive position occurs 1102 times, the second 543 times, and
the third only 10 times. It will thus be seen that the second
position, on an average, occurs only half as often as the first, and
that the third position is very rare. In ANDOCIDES the second
position preponderates, and in PS.-
DEM.49
it is used to the exclusion of the other two. The third position is
limited to ANTIPHON (thrice), LYSIAS (4 times), HYPERIDES (once),
AESCHINES (once), and DEMOSTHENES (once). The percentages for the
three positions are as follows: DIN. (only 28 instances) 82 / 18 /
0; LYCURG. (only 12 instances) 92 / 8 / 0; HYP. 53/ 42 / 5; DEM. 77/
23 / 0; [note: As a matter of fact, there is one
occurrence of the third position out of a total of 373
occurrences for the three positions.] PS.- DEM. 55/ 45 /
0; AESCHIN. 68/ 31 / 1; ISAE. 72 / 28 / 0; ISOC. 63/ 37 / 0; LYS.
71/ 27 / 1; ANDOC. 41/ 59 / 0; ANTIPHON 55 / 41 / 5. The two
extremes, not counting DINARCHUS and LYCURGUS, are ANDOCIDES with
only 41% of the first position and DEMOSTHENES with 77%.

XEN. An. With adjectives, the first position occurs 113 times, the
second position 13 times (Joost, p. 68), the third position 6 times
(ibid. p. 72). With possessives, the first position occurs 31 times,
the second position 4 times (l.c. p. 69), the third position once
(l.c. p. 72).

THUCYDIDES. With possessives, according to Dr. A. W. Milden, the
first position occurs 102 times in the speeches, the second position
3 times, and the third position 11 times.

HERODOTUS. According to Heikel, [note: I. A. Heikel, De
participiorum apud Herodotum usu, Helsingforsiae, 1884, p.
49] the first position occurs 98 times in the first book of
HERODOTUS, the second position 15 times, the third position 12
times.

ARISTOPHANES. The first position is more common than the second, the
third is comparatively rare.

EURIPIDES. In the Alcestis the first position predominates, the third
is not uncommon, the second very rare.

SOPHOCLES. In the Oedipus Tyrannus the first position is by far the
most common, the second is rare, the third is not uncommon.

AESCHYLUS. The second position is very rare, the first and third
positions correspond with the use of EURIPIDES and SOPHOCLES.

HOMER. The first position is common enough. The second position,
according to Krüger, is wanting. The third position, though
not uncommon with other adjectives, is rare with the possessive
pronoun.

§613

Rare attributive position.

There are a very few examples of the attributive use of the type

o( a)nh\r a)gaqo/s. Here the
adjective is appositive rather than predicative. In the Homeric
examples, some consider the article as still a demonstrative.

EUR. Hipp. 683-4:
*zeu/s s' o( (so the
Mss.; Wolff
se)
gennh/twr e)mo\s1 |
pro/rrizon e)ktri/yeien.

SOPH. Ai. 571-2:

kai\ ta)ma\ teu/xh mh/t' a)gwna/rxai tine\s

qh/sous' *)axaioi=s mh/q' o( [note: (so Mss.; Schaefer
mh/te）] lumew\n
e)mo/s

. [note: Both seem to be afterthoughts, both
represent the objective genitive. There is a proud appropriation
of
gennh/twr, an agonized
appropriation of
lumew/n, as if
mine, yes, mine.]

HOM. Od. 9.378:
o( moxlo\s e)la/i:nos
(cf. 375:
to\n moxlo/n), of
olive (and green olive to boot 379). 464:
ta\ mh=la, tanau/poda, pi/ona dhmw=|.
11.492:

paido/s, a)gauou=. 17.10:

§614

Attributive adverb.

The attributive position is the only one of which the adverb is
capable when combined directly with an articular substantive.

tai=s ... o)/ntws mai/ais,
PLATO, Theaet. 150A; Real
midwives.

th=s a)melei/as tau/ths th=s a)/gan,

DEM.4.17; This carelessness,
this over (carelessness).
de/ous kai\ taraxh=s th=s to/te,
LYS.[6] 35; The terror and unrest
then prevailing.

§615

Difference between attributive adverb and adjective.

The difference between the attributive adverb and the attributive
adjective is the difference between the participial element that the
adverb requires, and the adjective.

h( pro/teron o)ligarxi/a =
h( pro/teron ou)=sa o)ligarxi/a, the
oligarchy which formerly existed.

h( prote/ra o)ligarxi/a, cf.
LYS.12.65, the former of the two
oligarchies.

h( pro/teron gunh/, the one who
was formerly wife (now dead, divorced, or what not).

h( prote/ra gunh/, first wife,
former wife of two.

DIN.1.69:

tou\s pro/teron pole/mous (now at peace)

kalw=s diw/|khsas ... kata\ th\n prote/ran
ei)sfora/n (another pending).

§616

Attributive prepositional phrases.

Like the adverb, prepositional phrases are found in the three
attributive positions.

o( pro\s to\n ba/rbaron po/lemos,
DEM.14.32; The war with the
barbarian (the barbarian war).
to\n me\n po/lemon to\n pro\s basile/a,
14.9; The war, the (war in question）
with the king.

grafa\s ta\s e)c a)nqrw/pwn e)gra/feto,

LYS.13.73; He had a way of
framing indictments (that were) of
every earthly description.

§620

Attributive participle.

The nearer the participle is to an adjective, the more readily does
it take the normal attributive position of the adjective.

o( ... parw\n kairo/s,
DEM.1.2; The present opportunity.

ai( ... po/leis ai( dhmokratou/menai,

AESCHIN.3.6; The cities
that have a democratic form of government.

tro/pw| tw=| ei)rhme/nw|,
HDT.1.61; In the manner
specified.

§622

Participle or modifier outside of attributive complex.

When one or more modifiers are employed, the participle or the
modifier may be put outside of the complex.

h( ... e)n tw=| i)sqmw=| e)pimonh\
genome/nh,
THUC.2.18.3;
The delay on the Isthmus. (Cf. ibid.
h( e)n th=| *oi)no/h| e)pi/sxesis.)
to\n kateilhfo/ta ki/ndunon th\n po/lin,

DEM.18.220; The danger which
had come upon the city.

toi=s ei)rhme/nois tro/pois u(p' e)mou=,

LYS.12.77; After the manner
mentioned by me.

ti/nes ou)=n h(=san oi( para\ tou/tou lo/goi
to/te r(hqe/ntes;
DEM.18.35; What were the statements that were made by
him on that occasion?

## Predicative position of article, substantive, and adjective.

§627

When adjective and substantive are associated without the article, it
is a mere matter of inference whether the adjective is attributive
or predicative.
nau=s e)/rhmos may be
‘an empty ship’ or ‘a ship when
empty.’ In Il. 4.124:
kuklotere\s
me/ga to/con e)/teinen, He bent the great bow into
a round,

kuklotere/s is predicate,
me/ga is attribute. At this stage, then,
the Greek language presents the same state of things that is
familiar to us in Latin.

But when the substantive was used with the article and the adjective
without it, inasmuch as the articular substantive represented the
old notion and the adjective the new, a predicative relation was set
up. [note: Cf. THEON , Progymn. in RHET. GR., II,
83 (Sp.):
prosqe/sei a)/rqrwn ou)ke/ti
a)mfi/bolos gi/netai h( le/cis: ei)si\ de\ *ai)gu/ptioi oi(
*kolxoi/: dh=lon ga\r ge/gonen o(/ti peri\ *kolxw=n le/gei
w(/s ei)sin *ai)gu/ptioi.] As this position is
used only in a few combinations in English, it is often necessary to
translate by a relative clause or an abstract noun and often the
translation costs more than it comes to.

ou)/toi ta\ xrh/mat' i)/dia ke/kthntai
brotoi/, EUR. Phoen. 555; The property that mortals
hold is not their own.

o(/sa sumbebou/leuka ... a)p' ... a)diafqo/rou
th=s yuxh=s (sc.
sumbebou/leuka),
DEM.18.298;
All that I have counselled, I have counselled in the
innocency of my heart.

tou\s de\ taxei=an th\n a)poxw/rhsin
poihsame/nous,
LYCURG.96;
Those that took their departure quickly, made quick work
of their departure.

lampra=| th=| fwnh=|,
DEM.19.199; With his voice ringing
clear, with ringing voice.

mega/lh| th=| fwnh=|,
DEM.57.11; At the top of his voice,
with loud voice.

§628

Limitations of the predicative position.

The popular use of the predicative position seems never to have gone
far beyond the more simple combinations, and outside of these it is
found most frequently in authors who are given to making points and
so becomes a gnomon of artificial style. [note: A. J.
P. xvii (1896), 518. See also Milden, The Limitations of
the Predicative Position in Greek, Baltimore, 1900,
especially p. 43.]

thlikau/thn a)poxrh=n oi)=mai th\n
du/namin,
DEM.4.22; I
think that the force (proposed) is
sufficient (though) so small.

kai\ ga\r th=| le/cei poihtikwte/ra| kai\
poikilwte/ra| ta\s pra/ceis dhlou=si,
ISOC.15.47; For the language in
which they set forth their themes has a leaning toward the
poetical and ornate.

a)napeptame/nais au)to\n e)de/xonto tai=s
pu/lais, Ibid. 126; They received him with gates
wide open.

§629

Predicative position in phrases that have to do with the person.

In determining the stylistic effect of any given instance of the
predicative adjective with an articular noun, it must be borne in
mind that the predicative use was fostered by the employment of the
article as a possessive, and many of the most familiar turns consist
of phrases that have to do with the person, such as
mega/lh| th=| fwnh=|.

lampra=| th=| fwnh=|,
DEM.19.199; With his voice ringing
clear, with ringing voice (627).
mega/lh| th=| fwnh=|,
DEM.57.11; At the top of his voice, with loud
voice (627).

§630

Predicative position in prepositional phrases.

Especially worthy of note is the use of the predicative position in
combination with prepositions, a construction which adds to the
advantages of the genitive absolute that of greater exactness.

ei)s e(/rhmon th\n xw/ran,

; Into the country, stripped of its defenders.

a)p' . . . a)diafqo/rou th=s yuxh=s,

of my heart.

meta\ a)kraifnou=s th=s cummaxi/as,

THUC.1.19; With the alliance
in its integrity.

## Predicative positions.

§631

The first position in which the predicative adjective precedes the
articular noun is more common than the second position in which the
adjective follows.

§634

Participle in predicative position.

The predicative position is the most common position of the
participle. Instances of this position in which the participle is
the equivalent of an adjective, have been cited under the preceding
sections. Other examples will be given later under the head of
Predicative Attribution, the
Participle, the Compound Sentence, and the
Abridged Sentence. For the predicative position of
the attributive participle, see 622 and 623.

§635

Seeming attributive or predicative position of kalou/menos, o)nomazo/menos, w)/n, and
the like.

The articular copulative participle is regularly attracted into the
gender and number of the predicate (cf. 124.127 f., 492), and the
resulting positions merely simulate attribution or predication.

h( o)nomazome/nh a)ndrei/a, PLATO ,

what is now called the belly.

(so-called) pleasures.

§637

o(/los in predicative position.

o(/los prefers the first predicative
position with its decided emphasis. The second position is a manner
of afterthought.

642.
pa=s, a(/pas, su/mpas (
suna/pas). — The original
difference between
a(/pas and
pa=s gradually faded and the avoidance of
hiatus in artistic prose tended to prevent the employment of
a(/pas after a vowel. On the other hand,
after a consonant a dislike to
pa=s
began to manifest itself.
kalw= d' e)nanti/on
u(mw=n a)/ndres *)aqhnai=oi tou\s qeou\s a(/pantas (so
all MSS.)
kai\ p a/sas, says
Demosthenes, 18.141, though in the prooemium he had said
toi=s qeoi=s eu)/xomai pa=si kai\ pa/sais.
The extent of this discrimination according to position varies with
various authors and various spheres. The extremes are found in the
Pseudo-Xenophontean
*)aqhnai/wn
politei/a, which uses
pa=s and

a(/pas indifferently after vowels
and consonants, and in Isocrates, who applies the rule with great
rigor.

According to Diels, Gött. gel. Anz. 1894.297 ff., Isocrates
uses
pa=s 340 times, 287 times after a
vowel, and 53 times after a consonant;
a(/pas is used 528 times after a consonant and only 14
times after a vowel (12 times after
peri/ and twice after
pro/).
The 53 exceptional cases of the use of
pa=s after a consonant are due to other euphonic
considerations, to the fixity of stereotyped expressions, such as

e)k panto\s tro/pou, to faulty
tradition, or to lack of revision. The Panegyricus presents no
exception. For further details, see Diels, l.c.

Numerically
pa=s, as a rule, far
outweighs
a(/pas; a(/pas is much more
frequent than
su/mpas, and
suna/pas is rare. In Plato, according to
computations based upon the tables of Walbe, Syntaxis Platonicae
specimen, Bonn, 1888, pp. 4 sq.,
pa=s
occurs 5714 times,
a(/pas 834 times,

cu/mpas 372 times, and
cuna/pas 13 times. In Isocrates, however,

a(/pas is much more common than

pa=s (see above), and in Plato 's
Sophistes and Politicus
cu/mpas occurs
more than twice as often as
a(/pas (20:
8 and 45: 18).

§643

Position of pa=s, a(/pas, su/mpas.

pa=s, a(/pas, and
su/mpas (
suna/pas), like

o(/los, are used with articular and
with anarthrous substantives, and in predicative and attributive
position. The first predicative position is by far the most common.
Next in the order of frequency is the second predicative position.
This is the favorite position of
a(/pas
in some authors, and the regular position in
oi(
a)/lloi pa/ntes and kindred forms. The attributive
position comes last.

§644

Predicative position of pa=s, etc.

pa=s, a(/pas, su/mpas (
suna/pas) in predicative position are
generally used like the English all.

pa=s' h( boulh/,
DIN.1.15; All the senate.

pa=s' h( *(ella/s, Ibid. 31; All
Greece.

pa/ntes oi( poli=tai,
AESCHIN.3.8; All the citizens.

e)k tou= bi/ou panto\s kai\ tw=n e)/rgwn
tw=n tou= patro/s,

Lys. 19.13
; From my father's whole life and from his actions.

a(/pas o( dh=mos,
DEM.20.106; All the people.

o( dh=mos a(/pas, 21.2; The people
with one accord.

cu/mpan de\ to\ o(plitiko\n . . . trisxi/lioi
ma/lista,
THUC.4.124.1;
The aggregate (number of) the
hoplite contingent was approximately three thousand.

to\ d' o(plitiko\n cu/mpan h)qroi/sqh disxi/lioi
ma/lista,
THUC.5.6.5;
The heavy-armed troops that were mustered amounted in the
aggregate to about two thousand.

§646

a(/pas in Predicative Position:

DINARCHUS: 13 exx. of 1st pos. and 15 exx. of 2d pos. (including
1.106:
toi=s a)/llois a(/pasin
a)nqrw/pois).

LYCURGUS: 17 exx. of 1st pos. and 5 exx. of 2d pos., not counting 2
instances in a document.

HYPEREIDES: 8 exx. of 1st pos., including one doubtful one, and 11
exx. of 2d pos., including 2 exx. of the gen. and one of the
accusative of
oi( a)/lloi a(/pantes. Two
of the 11 exx. depend on restorations.

DEMOSTHENES: Of the singular of
a(/pas
there are in the Demosthenean corpus 33 exx. of the 1st pos and 23
exx. of the 2d. The latter exx. include articular proper names, 6
exx of the type
to\n a)/llon a(/panta
xro/non (9.22. [10], 51. 22.3. [42], 2. 57.47. 49), one

tou=ton a(/panta to\n xro/non
(19.57), and a group of three (38.7. 41.5. [49], 11), in which

a(/pas, while agreeing with the
substantive, is really a modifier of the predicate.

AESCHINES: 12 exx. of the first position, 15 of the second. These
include
sullh/bdhn a(/pantas (
3.18) and 3 exx. of

tou\s (
d'）

a)/llous a(/pantas.

ISOCRATES: 230 exx. of first position excluding
a(/pas with
a)/llos; 55

mh\ perii/dh|s th\n sautou= fu/sin a(/pasan a(/ma
dialuqei=san, but excluding
a(/pas with
a)/llos. In
combination with
a)/llos, a(/pas is
found 3 times in the type
a(pa/ntwn tw=n
a)/llwn, 35 times in the type
toi=s
a)/llois a(/pasin, 8 times as in
tw=n a)/llwn e)/rgwn a(pa/ntwn and 3 times as in

a(/pas with
a)/llos and 3 doubtful exx. In combination with
a)/llos, a(/pas once precedes (31.8:

tw=n a)/llwn
a(pa/ntwn, 2 times as in 19.64:

a)/llwn . . . a(pa/ntwn tw=n ei)rhme/nwn, and once as in

ANDOCIDES: 6 exx. of first position and 6 of second, besides 1.56:

PLATO. According to Walbe, l.c., pp. 13 and 31, Plato
uses
a(/pas and forms 101 times in the

(48 singulars). Like
pa=s, a(/pas
generally follows articular
a)/llos but
precedes articular
toiou=tos. ta)=lla
a(/panta (17),
oi( a)/lloi
a(/pantes (6),
a(/panta ta\
a)/lla (1); but
a(/panta ta\
toiau=ta (6),
a(/pantes oi(
toiou=toi (1),
ta\ toiau=ta
a(/panta (3). See Walbe, l.c., p. 32.

XENOPHON: According to Joost, l.c., pp. 78 f. there are
5 exx. of the first position of
a(/pas
(one plural) and 1 ex. (pl.) of the second position. In addition

§649

Attributive pa=s, etc.

pa=s in attributive position means
whole, whole number (amount）
of. It is often used in an absolute sense, all
that there is, are, as, for example,
o( pa=s xro/nos, eternity;

oi( pa/ntes a)/nqrwpoi, the whole
of mankind.

po/lis,

§650

§651

pa=s with anarthrous substantives.

With anarthrous singulars
pa=s means
every, every kind of; with abstracts, it is often
translated by perfect, complete; with anarthrous
plurals, it means all, all kinds of; but the
translations given do not exhaust the list of possible renderings.

Though the Greek differs from the English in the frequent use of the
article with the generic plural, and though this difference is not
effaced by the presence of
pa/ntes, e.g.
DEM. (7)
DEM., 3:

a(/pantes . . . oi( lh|stai/, all
pirates; 14.9:
tou\s . . . pole/mous
a(/pantas, all wars, yet the almost
invariable formula is
pa/ntes (
a(/pantes)
a)/nqrwpoi, all that are men, all men,
mankind, unless the whole of mankind is to be forcibly
contrasted with a portion of itself, in which case the attributive
form is used,
oi( pa/ntes a)/nqrwpoi
(649).

The partitive genitive plural accompanied by
pa=s regularly omits the article, except, of course, when
it is modified by another adjective or a participle, or is itself an
adjective or a participle, or designates not the whole genus but a
definite group thereof.

pa=s is rarely used, except in the neuter
plural, to modify an anarthrous adjective or participle used
substantively.

pa=s . . . a)nh/r,
PLATO, Legg. 856C; Every man,
everybody.

ste/fanos me\n a(/pas, ka)\n mikro\s
h)=|, DEM 22.75; A wreath of any kind, even if it be
small.

a(/pas . . . lo/gos,
DEM.2.12; Every assertion.

pa=san a(/deian,
DEM.19.149; Complete immunity.

a(/pasa . . . a)sfa/lei（
a), Ibid. 164; Perfect
(absolute) safety.

pa/sh| ponhri/a|,
DEM.25.80; With every kind of villainy.

pa/ntes a)/nqrwpoi,
DEM.23.123; All men, everybody.

a)/dhlon to\ me/llon a(/pasin
a)nqrw/pois,
DEM.15.21;
All men are in the dark as to the future.

pa=si/n ei)si pra/gmasi kai\ lo/gois du/o
prosqh=kai,
DEM.23.75;
Everything that is done or said admits of two
predications.

h(\ (sc.
*eu/nomi/a)
pa/sas kai\ po/leis kai\
xw/ras sw/|zei,
DEM.25.11;
Eunomia is the saviour of all cities and countries.

pasw=n a)\n ei)/h dustuxesta/th
gunaikw=n,

nomi/zete,

PLATO. There are nearly 1000 instances of anarthrous
pa=s, etc., and less than one fifth of
these follow the substantive. With but very few exceptions the
reason for the postposition is evident at first sight, the
prominence of the substantive causing
pa=s to take second place. Anarthrous participles or
adjectives other than
a)/llos are rarely
used with
pa=s, and substantives
modified by
pa=s do not often have
another attribute.
pa=sa is frequently
used in the sense of all, perfect, complete, with such
abstracts as
a)kri/beia, a)maqi/a, a)na/gkh,
a)pori/a, eu)daimoni/a, eu)/noia, h(donh/, paranomi/a,
parrhsi/a, proqumi/a, spoudh/, etc. Especially
noteworthy are the expressions
pa=s
a)nh/r, which occurs 57 times, but never
a)nh\r pa=s; pa=s a)/nqrwpos, 14 times, with
a)/nqrwpos pa=s, 3 times;
panti\ tro/pw|, 32 times;
pa/ntes (
a(/pantes)
a)/nqrwpoi and
forms, 52 times (4 exx. of second position). Cf. Walbe, l.c., pp. 19
f., 27.30 f.

§652

pa=s with substantives whose
article is implicit.

The use of
pa=s with a substantive whose
article is implicit is chiefly poetical except in phrases and
formulae, in enumerations, and with proper names. Comp. 536.538, and
568.

§653

Predicative position of pronominal adjectives.

au)to/s, a)/mfw and
a)mfo/teroi, e(/kastos, e(ka/teros, po/teros, o(po/teros,
ou)de/teros, and the three great demonstratives
o(/de, ou(=tos, and
e)kei=nos, assume the predicative position. In all
these, the first position is the normal one, the second position is
the afterthought position.

§660

e(/kastos.

A substantive qualified by
e(/kastos
regularly takes the article in prose, but in prepositional and
genitive expressions of time it is freely omitted. Isocrates seems
never to have omitted the article.

e)pei\ de\ fw=s e)ge/neto, h)=gen e(/kastos o(
strathgo\s to\n au(tou= lo/xon e)pi\ kw/mhn, XEN. An.
6.3.2; When it got light, each of the generals led his own
company to attack one of the villages.

e)ge/nonto me\n lo/xoi . . . a)mfi\ tou\s
o)gdoh/konta, o( de\ lo/xos e(/kastos sxedo\n ei)s tou\s
e(kato/n, Ibid. 4.8.15; There were about eighty
companies, each company averaging about a hundred men.

kaq' e(ka/sthn th\n h(me/ran,
ISOC.4.78.

h(me/ran, XEN. Hell. 2.1.27.

For further examples of the predicative position and for the use of
demonstratives with anarthrous substantives, see
Demonstratives. Compare also A. J. P. xxiii, 8.

§666

Use and omission of the article with the predicate.

As the article serves to point out that which is supposed to be
already present to the mind and the predicate generally introduces
something new, the article is not much used with the predicate
except in convertible propositions, that is, those statements in
which predicate may be subject or subject predicate. Otherwise the
article serves to quote or to refer.

tw=n ga\r o)/mbrwn kai\ tw=n au)xmw=n . . .

is to be regarded as the subject. Hence examples like

## Article with several attributes.

§669

When there are several attributes, the article may or may not be
repeated. If the article is not repeated, the second modifier may
follow the substantive, chiefly in poetry, and thus suggests a
predicative translation.

para\ th\n xalkh=n th\n mega/lhn *)aqhna=n
e)k decia=s e(/sthken,

DEM.19.272
; By the side of the tall bronze Athena and to her
right is standing (this inscription).

to\ tei=xos to\ makro\n to\ no/tion,

ANDOC.3.7
; The long wall on the South.

th\n i)di/an tu/xhn th\n e)mh/n,

DEM.18.255
; My own personal fortunes.

tw=n . . . e)pi\ tou= bh/matos par' u(mi=n
lo/gwn u(mei=s a)khko/ate,

AESCHIN.2.44
; The speeches on the bema delivered before you, you
have (already) heard.

to\n tou= e)pieikou=s mimhth\n a)/kraton,

PLATO, Rpb. 397D
; The uncompromising imitator of that which is fair and
reasonable.

§671

Repetition of Article, One of the Modifiers Following its
Substantive:

These examples present no peculiarity from the point of view of the
article. They show only the ordinary complex of the second
attributive position with an added modifier in the first half.

§674

Pseudo-attributive position of predicative adjectives.

Predicative adjectives limiting substantives that have one or more
attributive modifiers, often assume a seemingly attributive
position.

to\n a)/llon a(/panta xro/non,

DEM. 9.22
; In all the past.

th=s au)th=s tau/ths nukto/s,

THUC. 8.102.1
; That very night.

.

.

oi)kei/as xw/ras au)tou= stratei/as

o(mili/ai
